# Now, the Installation of Temples
<note>original: "atha devālayā -- nayanāni"</note>
Similarly, the consecration of water reservoirs <term>Jalashaya: public tanks, wells, or ponds</term> and gardens is highly recommended under various auspicious conditions. Some authorities have stated that the month of Chaitra <note>the first month of the Hindu calendar, roughly March-April</note> is inferior or undesirable for the act of consecration.

In the bright fortnight <term>Shukla Paksha: the waxing phase of the moon</term>, the following constellations <term>Nakshatras: lunar mansions</term> are auspicious for the ritual of flowers: Radha <note>original: "radhā"; an ancient name for the Vishakha constellation</note>, Rohini, Mrigashira, Shravana, the three *Uttaras* <note>Uttara Phalguni, Uttara Ashadha, and Uttara Bhadrapada</note>, Hasta, and Pushya. The installation is remembered as best when performed on the deity's own presiding weekday, constellation, and lunar day. For the principal gods, beginning with Brahma, one should specifically observe the strength of the rising sign <term>Lagna: the zodiac sign ascending on the eastern horizon</term>.

### Now, Specific Constellations for Specific Deities
Rohini, Shravana, Jyeshtha, Pushya, and Abhijit are prescribed for the proper installation of Vidhi <note>a name for Brahma, the Creator</note> and Vasava <note>a name for Indra, King of the Gods</note> during the month of Magha <note>roughly January-February</note>.

For the Sun, the constellation Hasta is used; for Kubera <note>God of Wealth</note> and Skanda <note>the Warrior God, also known as Kartikeya</note>, Anuradha is favored. Under the constellation Mula, one should install Durga and related goddesses. For Sugata <note>original: "sugata"; a title for the Buddha</note>, the constellation Ashlesha <note>OCR: "avale", likely "āśleṣe"</note> is specified.

Under Revati, one installs Dharma, Heramba <note>a five-faced form of Ganesha</note>, and the primary protectors <note>original: "rakṣasā"; likely referring to guardian spirits or Nairrta</note>. For the devotees of the sages and for Valmiki, Pushya is recommended. For the Seven Sages <term>Saptarishi: the seven stars of the Big Dipper</term> and for Yogis, the installation should occur on their specific lunar days. For all deities in general, Rohini and the three *Uttara* constellations are suitable. The installation of the Lords of the Directions <term>Digishas: the guardians of the eight compass points</term> is declared to be best under the Dhanistha constellation.

### Now, Specific Rising Signs for Specific Deities
The Sun and Shiva should be installed when Leo is the rising sign. Vishnu <note>original: "hari"</note> should be installed in a feminine sign <note>even-numbered zodiac signs like Taurus, Cancer, etc.</note>. Brahma <note>original: "vedhā"</note> belongs in Aquarius; Rudra in a movable sign; Goddesses in dual-natured signs; and all deities may be installed in fixed signs.
<gloss>| 30 |</gloss>

### Now, the Results of the Weekdays
The results of the days [of the week] are described: some are harsh at the beginning or end... <unclear>vārudā vara rāha</unclear>... others are givers of joy and provide stability for a long time <note>original: "ānanda dāyinī kalpa sthāpi"</note>... 
<gloss>| 31 |</gloss>
...on certain days.

### Now, Specific Months for Specific Deities
Even during the Southern Transit of the Sun <term>Dakshinayana: the period between the summer and winter solstices</term>, a wise person may install the following: Varaha <note>the Boar avatar of Vishnu</note>, the Mother Goddesses <note>original: "mātṛ"</note>, Bhairava <note>a fierce manifestation of Shiva</note>, Vamana <note>the Dwarf avatar of Vishnu</note>, the Slayer of the Buffalo Demon <note>original: "mahiṣā sura hannī"; Mahishasura-mardini or Durga</note>, and Narasimha <note>the Man-Lion avatar of Vishnu</note>. One should install the Linga <note>the symbolic form of Shiva</note> in the month of Shravana, and Jagadambika <note>the Mother of the World</note> in the month of Ashvina.

It then transitions into a new section on "Miscellaneous Matters," specifically focusing on *Anga Sphurana* (the divination of involuntary body twitches), a traditional form of omens (shakuna) used to predict future events based on physiological sensations.</meta>

...[consecration]. In the month of Margashirsha <note>roughly November-December</note>, it is recommended to install Hari <note>a name for Vishnu</note>. Some authorities suggest that all deities may be installed during the month of Pausha <note>roughly December-January</note>.

### The Strength of the Ascendant for Installation
Now, we consider the strength of the rising sign <term>Lagna: the zodiac sign appearing on the eastern horizon at the time of the ritual</term> for a consecration. Benefic planets <note>such as Jupiter, Venus, or a strong Moon</note> should be placed in the "centers" or "corners" <term>Kendra and Kona: the 1st, 4th, 7th, 10th and 5th, 9th houses of a birth chart</term>. Malefic planets <note>such as Mars or Saturn</note> should ideally be placed in the "upachaya" houses <note>the 3rd, 6th, and 11th houses, where their negative influence is neutralized</note>. When deities are installed while the foundations of the heavens are thus aligned, they bestow great prosperity upon the devotee.

Thus ends the twentieth chapter of the **Muhurta Ganapati**, composed by the son of the illustrious astrologer Ravala Shankara, concerning the installation of water reservoirs, gardens, and deities.

# The Miscellaneous Chapter
### First: The Section on Body Twitching

I shall now describe the effects of involuntary body twitches <term>Anga Sphurana: a traditional divinatory practice</term>. It should be understood that for men, a twitch originating on the right side of the body is auspicious, whereas for women, the same is true for the left side.

*   **Top of the Head:** Gain of land.
*   **Forehead:** Gain of status or a new position.
*   **Between the Eyebrows:** Meeting with a loved one.
*   **Both Eyebrows:** Great happiness.
*   **Ears:** Hearing auspicious news.
*   **Eyes:** Seeing something or someone dear.
*   **Corners of the Eyes:** Obtaining wealth <note>original: "lakshmī"</note>.
*   **Lower Eyelid:** A victory or success.
*   **Cheeks:** Happiness related to a woman.
*   **Nose:** Joy derived from pleasant fragrances.
*   **Upper Lip:** An argument or debate.
*   **Lower Lip:** <insert>Kissing</insert> a woman.
*   **Jaw/Chin:** A sense of fear.
*   **Mouth:** Enjoying a sweet or delicious meal.
*   **Throat (front):** Acquisition of land.
*   **Back of the Neck:** Fear arising from enemies.
*   **Throat (general):** Victory or defeat <note>the text implies the outcome depends on the specific sensation</note>.
*   **Shoulders:** Meeting with friends.
*   **Arms:** Meeting with a loved one.
*   **Middle of the Arm:** The arrival of wealth.
*   **Hands:** Gaining money.
*   **Finger Joints:** Victory.
*   **Wrists:** Joy and delight.
*   **Palms:** Obtaining <unclear>an excellent</unclear> woman.
*   **Armpits:** Movement away from one's current location, or an increase in grief.
*   **Heart:** Obtaining <insert>objects of desire</insert>.
*   **Hips:** Union with a husband <note>intended for female subjects</note>.
*   **Buttocks:** Obtaining a vehicle.
*   **Male Genitals:** Union with a woman.
*   **Testicles:** The birth or gain of a son.
*   **Bladder/Groin Area:** General prosperity and rise in status.
*   **Thighs:** Obtaining new clothing.
*   **Knees:** Conflict or peace with an enemy.
*   **Shanks (Lower Leg):** Some form of loss.
*   **Top of the Feet:** Gain of a new position.
*   **Soles of the Feet:** Gains through travel or a journey.

On the left side...

<meta>This page continues the "Miscellaneous Chapter" of the *Muhurta Ganapati*. Having introduced the general concept of involuntary body twitches (Anga Sphurana) on the previous page, this section provides specific results for different body parts, introduces the omens associated with itching and lizards falling on the body, and begins the classification of dreams.</meta>

*   For men, the wise declare that the fruits of twitching on the right side of the body are auspicious; for women, the opposite is true <note>i.e., the left side is auspicious for women</note>.

### On Itching and Specific Twitches
**The results of itching** <term>Kandutvam: an itching sensation</term> should be understood as identical to those of twitching.
*   A twitch in the right hand is said to bring wealth or success for kings.
*   For others, it indicates various gains.
*   A twitch in the sole of the foot indicates that one will soon undertake a journey.

### On the Falling of a Lizard
Now, we consider the **omens of a lizard falling** <term>Palli-patana: the ancient practice of divining the future based on where a lizard lands on a person</term>. One must consider the lunar date, the day of the week, and the lunar mansion <term>Nakshatra: the 27 sectors of the sky the moon passes through</term> at the time of the fall.

The following lunar mansions are considered auspicious for a lizard fall: Pushya, Ashvini, Rohini, Mrigashirsha, Punarvasu, the three "Uttara" signs <note>Uttara Phalguni, Uttara Ashadha, and Uttara Bhadrapada</note>, Dhanishtha, Anuradha, and Shatabhisha. Other mansions are generally considered forbidden or inauspicious.

As with twitches, a lizard falling on the right side of the body is auspicious for men, and on the left side for "deer-eyed" women <note>original: "mrigidrsha"; a common poetic Sanskrit term for beautiful women</note>. Specifically, a lizard falling on the navel, the heart, the stomach, or the head is considered to bring good fortune. Falling on other parts is not considered auspicious.

### Remedies for Inauspicious Omens
If a lizard falls during an inauspicious time or on an unlucky part of the body—such as during an "unbalanced" astrological rising sign <term>Lagna</term> or when the moon is in the eighth house—misfortune may arise. To counter this, one should perform a "peace ritual" <term>Shanti: a propitiatory rite to neutralize bad omens</term>. This includes:
*   Chanting sacred prayers <term>Japa</term>.
*   Fire sacrifices <term>Homa</term>.
*   Reciting the Rudra prayers <note>hymns to Shiva</note>.
*   Bathing in "five products of the cow" <term>Panchagavya: a purifying mixture of milk, curd, ghee, urine, and dung</term>.
*   Looking at one’s reflection in clarified butter <note>a traditional method of "giving away" bad luck</note>.

### On the Interpretation of Dreams
Now, the **results of seeing dreams** <term>Svapna-darshana</term>. The time at which a dream occurs determines when its results will manifest:
1.  **First Watch** <note>roughly 6:00 PM to 9:00 PM</note>: The result will manifest within one year.
2.  **Second Watch** <note>9:00 PM to Midnight</note>: The result will manifest within six months.
3.  **Third Watch** <note>Midnight to 3:00 AM</note>: The result will manifest within three months.
4.  **At Dawn**: The result will manifest immediately or very soon.

Dreams can lead to liberation from disease or the acquisition of wealth.

### Auspicious Dream Figures
The following figures appearing in a dream are considered bringers of good fortune and prosperity:
*   The King, Brahmins <note>the priestly class</note>, or Deities.
*   Perfected beings <term>Siddhas</term>, celestial musicians <term>Gandharvas</term>, or spirits <term>Kinnaras</term>.
*   One’s Teacher <term>Guru</term>.
*   A woman dressed in white.
*   Mounting an elephant or a horse...

<meta>This page continues the specific categorization of dream results (Svapna-phala) within the "Miscellaneous Chapter" of the Muhurta Ganapati. While the previous page focused on positive symbols like white animals and ritual objects, this page expands on those who appear in dreams (such as authority figures) and introduces inauspicious omens like nakedness or falling into mud.</meta>

<margin>4.
Now, concerning dreams...
<unclear>...</unclear></margin>
One attains a kingdom <note>original: "rājamadhigacchati"</note> [if one sees] a cow, a bearer of a sectarian mark <term>Liṅgī: a person wearing the outward symbols or robes of a specific religious order</term>, a Brahmana <term>Brāhmaṇa: a member of the priestly or scholarly class</term>, a king, a father, a friend, or a deity. In the two-fold nature of dreams—both true and false—when such things are seen, [success] indeed arises. Seeing a Shiva-linga <note>original: "śivalingaṃ"; the aniconic representation of the god Shiva</note>, a deity, or a hidden treasure being found gives men wealth and prosperity. Apart from seeing cotton, all these are considered auspicious. All things combined with cows, gods, elephants <term>Gaja: the elephant, often symbolizing strength and royal fortune</term>, and the "twice-born" <note>original: "dvija"; a term for members of the three upper social classes, particularly Brahmanas</note> bring gain. However, to see oneself naked <note>original: "digvāsā"; literally "sky-clad"</note>, or riding upon a cow, a buffalo, a camel, a donkey, or a bull—

---
<note>A thick dark horizontal smudge separates the sections of the manuscript here.</note>

—and [eating] meat, [seeing] a woman who has recently given birth <note>original: "sūtikā"</note>, or seeing those who are mutilated; these are considered signs of release from poison. When saffron, vermilion, or minerals are seen falling in one's house, that house will suffer from fire or theft. [Dreams of] things emerging from the nose, or the best of gems and gold, [are good]; but traveling by a vehicle drawn by donkeys, camels, or spirits <note>original: "piśāca"; often translated as flesh-eating demons or spirits</note> is a difficult omen. Being smeared with mud or mire, or sinking into mud, leads to poverty and sorrow. The falling of teeth or being cast upwards—these are sorrow-bearing dreams, and when seen, they are known to cause loss. Singing, swinging, and the music of a lute <term>Vīṇā: a traditional Indian stringed instrument</term> are described by the very wise as [signs of] being brought under the power of others or going downward. If one sees the sun, or a crown placed upon one's head, or the wearing of a white garment, it signifies an increase in status. However, [seeing] a village or chariot [in a specific state] may indicate misfortune. To see the Karavira <term>Karavīra: the Oleander plant</term> or the Ashoka tree <term>Aśoka: a tree (Saraca asoca) famed for its beautiful red flowers and its name, which means "without sorrow"</term>, or to be bound by creepers and ropes, or to be embraced by...

<summary>This page details various dream omens, distinguishing between auspicious figures like kings and priests and inauspicious actions like riding donkeys or sinking in mud. It provides a systematic guide for interpreting the symbolic landscape of the sleeping mind in early modern India.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the list of dream interpretations (Svapna-shastra) from the previous page. While the previous section ended with the inauspicious omen of riding certain animals or being naked, this page transitions into more specific visual omens, ranging from climbing trees to encountering ghosts and various metals.</meta>

<margin>(4) 47</margin>
...[One who is] climbing <note>original: "āruhya"</note> trees that are in full bloom, or one who sees [such trees] <note>original: "vividhāni"</note>... 23. By obtaining fruits or seeing sons [in a dream], one becomes purified. However, the one who sees blood <note>original: "raktaṃ"</note> or the killing of a sectarian practitioner <term>Liṅgī: a person wearing the outward symbols or robes of a specific religious order</term> obtains [the sin of] murder. A man who sees himself becoming very small, or who does not see his own reflection while traveling—by that [sign], he is not granted [good fortune] and is not purified. Flies, scorpions, and biting insects seen in a dream are not auspicious <note>original: "na śobhanāḥ"</note>. 54. Among gems, weapons, shoes, or kings, and among women, clothes, and other dear objects—he who is a thief [of these in a dream] goes to hell. In a dream, the falling of eyes <note>original: "netra ruk patane"</note>, or the falling into a well <note>original: "kūpa prapatane"</note>, or into caves and dark crevices, is not auspicious. One should not see an owl <note>original: "kausika"</note>, a donkey, a crow <note>original: "vāyasaḥ"</note>, a jackal <note>original: "śṛgāla"</note>, a boar <note>original: "vārāha"</note>, or a dog; these are not auspicious when seen in a dream. Similarly, [seeing] a fish scale or a piece of a fish <unclear>...</unclear> in water, or drinking water in a place where there is cow dung, or drinking yellow liquid on dry land, is not auspicious. One who finds himself in a hellish pit of feces or pus <note>original: "purīṣa pūye"</note> will obtain [sickness] and will reside in a place of disease and division. One who dies while performing ancestral rites <term>Pitṛ-kārya: religious rituals and offerings made to honor deceased ancestors</term>—of this there is no doubt—

<margin>45</margin>
—or who associates with ghosts <term>Bhūta: a spirit or ghost of a deceased person</term>, spirits <term>Piśāca: a flesh-eating demon or malevolent spirit</term>, and outcasts <note>original: "śvapaca"; literally "dog-cooker," referring to those outside the traditional social order</note>, or who is struck or bound by the noose of death, [that person] will surely die. Seeing the sun during the day or night, or seeing poison, a fierce fire, or a falling meteor—one who sees such terrors will perish and meet his end. Seeing lead <note>original: "sīsa"</note>, brass <note>original: "pittala"</note>, musk <note>original: "kastūrī"</note>, bronze <note>original: "kāṃsya"</note>, copper <note>original: "tāmra"</note>, iron, or tin <note>original: "trapu"</note>, or seeing dried herbs or a craftsman <note>original: "śilpī"</note>—these are not auspicious when seen in a dream.
